## Reasoning

Let me analyze the mission and the champion:

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for delivering the "good" item (one half of source)
- Score increases by 1 for delivering the "bad" item (other half)
- Need to: go to the correct half of the source (e.g., BLUE side), pick up item, return to nest (WHITE floor), deliver
- Nest = WHITE floor, RED wall; Source = BLACK floor (BLUE or GREEN walls)
- Gray = in between

**Champion Analysis:**
- State 0: Exploration (B=0, rwm=74), 4 transitions:
  - neighbors below 8 (isolated) → State 1
  - perceives color 4 (yellow?) → State 1
  - gray floor → State 1
  - neighbors below 1 → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor (B=8, clr=2=green), toward GREEN
  - neighbors below 8 → back to State 0
  - black floor → State 0 (prob 0.0865)
  - black floor → State 0 (prob 0.2499)

The champion goes toward GREEN (clr=2) which might be the good/bad side. Score is -4.20 (negative = good since we minimize).

**Strategy:**
- The robot needs to go to one color source, pick up item, return to nest
- Key: must distinguish which half gives good vs bad item
- Colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan
- Wall colors in arena: RED (nest side), BLUE and GREEN (source halves)

**Better design ideas:**
1. Explore → detect BLACK floor (source) → go to nest (WHITE/RED wall) → repeat
2. Use 3-4 states: Explore → GoToColor(blue or green) → GoToColor(red=nest) → back
3. The champion goes toward green (clr=2). Let's try going toward blue (clr=3) as alternative.

For a foraging FSM:
- State 0: Explore (find source)
- State 1: GoToColor toward source color (blue or green)
- State 2: GoToColor toward RED (nest wall) to deliver
- State 3: Exploration back

Let me design variants:

**Machine 1 (SURVIVAL - exact copy of champion):**
As given.

**Machine 2 (exploit - shift probabilities):**
Similar to champion but tweak probabilities.

**Machine 3 (exploit - try blue instead of green):**
Same topology but clr=3 (blue).

**Machine 4-14 (explore - different topologies):**
3-4 state machines with proper foraging behavior.

Key insight: GoToColor with clr=2 (green) means going toward GREEN walls = one source half. When on BLACK floor (at source), go toward RED (nest). This is a good foraging loop.

Let me design a proper 3-state forager:
- S0: Exploration (random walk to find source)
- S1: GoToColor(GREEN or BLUE) - approach source
- S2: GoToColor(RED) - return to nest

Transitions:
- S0→S1: perceive color 2 (green) or on gray floor
- S1→S2: on black floor (at source, picked up item)
- S2→S0: on white floor (delivered item)
- S2→S1: perceive green (went wrong direction, saw source)

For the 3-state machines, I need to be careful about destination indexing. With 3 states (0,1,2), leaving state 0 the others are indexed as 0→state1, 1→state2. Leaving state 1: 0→state0, 1→state2. Leaving state 2: 0→state0, 1→state1.

Let me construct carefully:

- S0: Explore (rwm)
- S1: GoToColor(green, clr=2) 
- S2: GoToColor(red/nest, clr=1)

S0 transitions: on black→S1, perceive green→S1, prob→S1
S1 transitions: on black floor→S2, on white→S2
S2 transitions: on white→S0, perceive green→S1

**4-state forager:**
- S0: Explore

- S2: GoToColor(red=1, nest)
- S3: Exploration (different rate)

## Reasoning

**Champion Analysis:**
The champion is a 2-state machine:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=74), with 4 transitions all going back to itself... wait, all destinations are 0 (same state)? That can't be right with rule 4 ("Never point a transition at its own state").

Wait, re-reading: with 2 states, leaving state 0, the "other" states are indexed as D=0→state1, D=1→state0... No wait. The format says "D=0 targets state 0" for the destination index among OTHER states. With 2 states leaving state 0, there's only state 1 as "other", so D=0 means state 1.

So the champion:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=74), 4 transitions all going to D=0 = State 1:
  - neighbors below 8 → State 1
  - perceives yellow (color 4) → State 1  
  - gray floor → State 1
  - neighbors below 1 → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(green, clr=2), 3 transitions all going to D=0 = State 0:
  - neighbors below 8 → State 0
  - black floor (prob 0.0865) → State 0
  - black floor (prob 0.2499) → State 0

So the robot explores, then when it detects yellow LEDs / gray floor / isolated, goes to GoToGreen. When on black floor (source), returns to exploring. This seems like it's going toward the green source and picking up items, then going back to explore (which might drift toward the nest).

The score is -4.28. This is decent but we need to do better.

**Key insight:** The robot needs to:
1. Go to one source half (good one)
2. Return to nest (WHITE floor, RED wall)

The champion goes toward GREEN (clr=2). When it hits black floor, it goes back to exploration. Exploration with rwm=74 is fairly random. This might work but isn't very directed for returning.

**Better strategy:** After hitting black floor (got item), go toward RED wall (nest). Let me design 3-state machines that do this explicitly.

For 3-state FSM, destination indexing:
- From state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1

For 4-state FSM:
- From state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2, D=2→state3
- From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2, D=2→state3
- From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→state3
- From state 3: D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→state2

**Design plan:**
1. SURVIVAL: exact copy of champion
2-5. EXPLOIT: small tweaks to champion (different rwm, probabilities, steepness)
6-7. EXPLOIT: champion topology but clr=3 (blue) 
8-10. EXPLORE: 3-state (explore → GoToGreen → GoToRed)
11-12. EXPLORE: 3-state (explore → GoToBlue → GoToRed)
13-14. EXPLORE: 4-state or different 2-state designs

Let me be careful with the 3-state machines:

**3-state forager:**
- S0: Explore
  - perceive green → S1 (D=0)
  - black floor → S1 (D=0)
- S1: GoToColor(green, clr=2)
  - black floor → S2 (D=1) [at source, now return]
  - white floor → S2 (D=1) [at nest, already delivered]
- S2: GoToColor(red, clr=1) [go to nest]
  - white floor → S0 (D=0) [delivered]
  - perceive green → S1 (D=1) [see source again]

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion is unchanged at -4.95.** We've been stuck for 2 rounds. Time to both preserve the champion and explore more aggressively.

**Champion structure recap:**
- S0: Explore(rwm=50), triggers on: neighbors<5, sees yellow, gray floor(0.95), black floor(0.85)→S2
- S1: GoToGreen(clr=2), triggers on: black→S2, white→S0, neighbors<5→S0
- S2: GoToRed(clr=1), triggers on: white→S0, sees green→S1, gray(0.1)→S0

**Why we might be stuck:**
1. The gray→S1 transition (prob 0.95) means robots almost always go to GoToGreen when on gray. But S1 has white→S0 which resets when near nest. This might cause robots to oscillate near the middle.
2. The "perceives green→S1" in S2 might interrupt delivery runs.
3. The gray floor transition in S2 (prob 0.1) is very weak - robots rarely leave GoToRed on gray.

**New ideas to try:**
1. Remove the "perceives green→S1" from S2 (let robots commit to delivery)
2. Make S2 transition on white floor with very high probability (0.99)
3. Try removing the gray→S0 from S2 entirely (just white→S0 and perceives green→S1)
4. Try a version where S1 has no white→S0 (robots don't abandon green approach near nest)
5. Try neighbors<2 threshold (even more isolated = go get item)
6. Try rwm=30 or 35 (even less random turning)
7. 4-state with explicit "return loaded" state that only exits on white
8. Try w0x0=4.0 or 5.0 (sharper sigmoid)
9. Try making S0 transition to S2 directly on black with prob 0.95 (higher)
10. Add perceives-red in S1 to help find nest after collecting

**Key insight:** The champion's S2 has "perceives green→S1" which might cause robots to abandon delivery when they see the green wall. This could be hurting performance. Let me try versions without this.

For 3-state destinations: From s0: D=0→s1, D=1→s2. From s1: D=0→s0, D=1→s2. From s2: D=0→s0, D=1→s1.

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Tweak white floor prob in S2 (0.95, 0.99)
2. Tweak gray floor prob in S2 (0.05, 0.1, 0.12)
3. Tweak black floor prob in S0 (0.88, 0.9, 0.92)
4. Tweak gray floor prob in S0 (0.92, 0.97, 0.99)
5. Tweak neighbor threshold (3, 4, 5=champion, 6)
6. Tweak rwm0 (35, 40, 45, 55)
7. Add a 3rd transition to S2 (e.g., perceives red → stay, or fixed prob)
8. Tweak S1 transitions

**Exploration ideas:**
1. 4-state with S2 only having white→S0
2. Remove gray→S0 from S2 entirely (only white exit)
3. Try S2 with 1 transition (only white floor)
4. Try with clr=3 (blue) instead of green
5. Different S0 structure

**Critical test:** What if S2 has ONLY the white floor transition? Robots would be stuck in GoToRed until they hit white. This might work better or cause issues if they get trapped.

For 3-state destinations: From s0: D=0→s1, D=1→s2. From s1: D=0→s0, D=1→s2. From s2: D=0→s0, D=1→s1.

## Reasoning

**New Champion (-6.56)! Excellent improvement!**

The winning variant from round 8 has S2 with:
- white floor → S0 (prob 0.95) 
- gray floor → S0 (prob 0.1)

vs the previous champion which had (0.97, 0.08). Very similar but slightly different probabilities. The key structure is confirmed: **no "perceives green" in S2** - robots commit to delivery.

**Champion structure:**
- S0: Explore(rwm=50), 4 transitions → S1 (neighbors<5, sees yellow, gray 0.95), → S2 (black 0.85)
- S1: GoToGreen(clr=2), 3 transitions → S2 (black 0.9), → S0 (white 0.9, neighbors<5)
- S2: GoToRed(clr=1), 2 transitions → S0 (white 0.95), → S0 (gray 0.1)

**What's working:** The 3-state loop is confirmed optimal topology. GoToGreen → GoToRed → Explore is the right cycle.

**Fine-tuning opportunities:**
1. S2 white floor prob: try 0.93, 0.97, 0.99
2. S2 gray floor prob: try 0.05, 0.08, 0.12, 0.15 (or remove entirely - try 1 transition only)
3. S0 black floor prob: try 0.8, 0.88, 0.9, 0.92
4. S0 gray floor prob: try 0.92, 0.97, 0.99
5. S1 black floor prob: try 0.85, 0.92, 0.95
6. S1 white floor prob: try 0.85, 0.92, 0.95
7. Neighbor threshold: try 3, 4 (currently 5)
8. rwm0: try 35, 40, 45, 55, 60
9. S1 neighbor threshold: currently 5, try 3, 4
10. Try S2 with only 1 transition (white floor only)

**Key experiments:**
- Remove gray from S2 entirely (1 transition only)
- Try slightly higher/lower probabilities systematically
- Try threshold=4 or 3

For 3-state destinations: From s0: D=0→s1, D=1→s2. From s1: D=0→s0, D=1→s2. From s2: D=0→s0, D=1→s1.

# PROMPT SYSTEME

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
